I mean, I probably put things off sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it avoiding whole situations. Sorry, that’s not exactly the same thing. I guess I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding specific places or activities because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worries and jumping thoughts are significantly affecting your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I kept wanting to say it’s just stress or I’m just behind, but I actually missed an internal lab deadline because I couldn’t get my writing and data review organized. I had the data, mostly, but I kept circling back through it and then the report just… didn’t come together.\n\nAnd it’s not only work. Even basic stuff feels like too much lately—getting reports ready, showing up for art nights with friends, cooking for my family. I’ll mean to do it, and then my head is full of all the things I need to sort out, and suddenly I’m behind on everything. So yeah, it’s affecting my life more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you're feeling anxious or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed, like, obvious sweating or trembling. Racing heart maybe? But I also drink too much coffee sometimes, especially when I’m trying to finish lab notes, so then I start wondering if it’s anxiety or just caffeine or because I skipped lunch, which I know is not helpful.\n\nWhen I’m overwhelmed it’s more like I realize I’m tense or kind of wound up, but I don’t always stop to check what my body is doing. So… I don’t know if I can give you a clear yes on those specific things.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually find that your worries are about a wide range of everyday things, or are they more focused on specific areas like work or school?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just one thing. Yeah, I think it started around the funding shift at the lab… when they brought in that commercial partnership and everything about how things were being run kind of changed.

It’s hard to pin it to one exact day, because at first I thought I was just adjusting, like everyone was. But it’s been about seven months now, and since then my thoughts have just been… jumping more, and I keep feeling like I can’t get settled. So, yeah, I’d say that’s when it really started.

I mean, I’ll look back at something at work because I’m worried I missed something, but then I get pulled into thinking about deadlines or whether people are annoyed with me, or—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed myself needing to repeat some exact action over and over.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I probably put things off sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it avoiding whole situations. Like, I still go to work and do the lab stuff, even if I’m kind of in my head about it.\n\nSometimes I’ll take longer to start something because I’m thinking through everything that could be wrong with it, and then I end up reorganizing my notes or answering some email that isn’t even urgent. Sorry, that’s not exactly the same thing. I guess I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding specific places or activities because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worries and jumping thoughts are significantly affecting your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I kept wanting to say it’s just stress or I’m just behind, but I actually missed an internal lab deadline because I couldn’t get my writing and data review organized. I had the data, mostly, but I kept circling back through it and then the report just… didn’t come together.\n\nAnd it’s not only work. Even basic stuff feels like too much lately—getting reports ready, showing up for art nights with friends, cooking for my family. I’ll mean to do it, and then my head is full of all the things I need to sort out, and suddenly I’m behind on everything. So yeah, it’s affecting my life more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you're feeling anxious or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed, like, obvious sweating or trembling. Racing heart maybe? But I also drink too much coffee sometimes, especially when I’m trying to finish lab notes, so then I start wondering if it’s anxiety or just caffeine or because I skipped lunch, which I know is not helpful.\n\nWhen I’m overwhelmed it’s more like I realize I’m tense or kind of wound up, but I don’t always stop to check what my body is doing. So… I don’t know if I can give you a clear yes on those specific things.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually find that your worries are about a wide range of everyday things, or are they more focused on specific areas like work or school?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just one thing. Work is a big piece of it, like I keep thinking the lab’s new partnership might somehow mean my position gets cut, or that my data are wrong and I’ve missed something obvious.\n\nBut then it jumps—like, bills, whether they’re going to pile up, whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, and then I start thinking I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school. And then I’m checking emails or rereading numbers, and it all kind of blends together. So… yeah, it’s a pretty wide range, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a particular event or experience that you think might have triggered the start of these worries and jumping thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it started around the funding shift at the lab… when they brought in that commercial partnership and everything about how things were being run kind of changed.\n\nIt’s hard to pin it to one exact day, because at first I thought I was just adjusting, like everyone was. But it’s been about seven months now, and since then my thoughts have just been… jumping more, and I keep feeling like I can’t get settled. I remember even small procedure changes turning into these long conversations in my head about what it meant, and then I’d go home and still be thinking about it while trying to do normal stuff. So, yeah, I’d say that’s when it really started.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… I mean, kind of. It’s less like I’m actively saying, “I’m not going to social things,” and more like everything feels like too much to keep up with, so I just… don’t go.

It’s kind of constant. I mean, I try to focus, but it just keeps moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do these jumping thoughts ever get stuck on a single idea or worry that you feel compelled to think about over and over?\n\nPatient: Not usually one single idea, I guess. Not really, no. I mean, even when I’m not physically at the lab or nobody’s talking about the partnership, my mind just keeps jumping around.

It’ll start with, like, “What if the new partnership means my position gets cut?” but then it’s suddenly, “What if my data are wrong?” and then I’m thinking about bills piling up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind. And then somehow I’m on whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school, even though I might just be, I don’t know, brushing my teeth or trying to eat dinner.

So it doesn’t feel like it improves much just because the lab stuff isn’t right in front of me. It follows me around.

It’s kind of constant. It’s more like I try to tell myself, okay, just focus on finishing this report, or just check the assay in front of you. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because my mind just won’t stop jumping around. Like, one minute I’m thinking the new partnership at our lab is going to mean my position gets cut, and then I’m worried my data are wrong, and then it’s bills, and whether my family thinks I’m falling behind.\n\nAnd then somehow that turns into me thinking I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school. It’s kind of constant. I mean, I try to focus, but it just keeps moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do these jumping thoughts ever get stuck on a single idea or worry that you feel compelled to think about over and over?\n\nPatient: Not usually one single idea, I guess. It’s more like I try to tell myself, okay, just focus on finishing this report, or just check the assay in front of you. But then one worry kicks off another, and suddenly I’m staring at the screen while my head is going through every possible way things could go wrong.\n\nLike it starts with the report being late, then maybe the results are off, then what if someone notices, and then it’s not even about the report anymore. It just keeps branching.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do something specific over and over, like checking or repeating certain actions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? Not in a... ritual kind of way, if that’s what you mean. I mean, I’ll look back at something at work because I’m worried I missed something, but then I get pulled into thinking about deadlines or whether people are annoyed with me, or—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed myself needing to repeat some exact action over and over.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I probably put things off sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it avoiding whole situations. Like, I still go to work and do the lab stuff, even if I’m kind of in my head about it.\n\nSometimes I’ll take longer to start something because I’m thinking through everything that could be wrong with it, and then I end up reorganizing my notes or answering some email that isn’t even urgent. Sorry, that’s not exactly the same thing. I guess I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding specific places or activities because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worries and jumping thoughts are significantly affecting your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I kept wanting to say it’s just stress or I’m just behind, but I actually missed an internal lab deadline because I couldn’t get my writing and data review organized. I had the data, mostly, but I kept circling back through it and then the report just… didn’t come together.\n\nAnd it’s not only work. Even basic stuff feels like too much lately—getting reports ready, showing up for art nights with friends, cooking for my family. I’ll mean to do it, and then my head is full of all the things I need to sort out, and suddenly I’m behind on everything. So yeah, it’s affecting my life more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you're feeling anxious or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed, like, obvious sweating or trembling. Racing heart maybe? But I also drink too much coffee sometimes, especially when I’m trying to finish lab notes, so then I start wondering if it’s anxiety or just caffeine or because I skipped lunch, which I know is not helpful.\n\nWhen I’m overwhelmed it’s more like I realize I’m tense or kind of wound up, but I don’t always stop to check what my body is doing. So… I don’t know if I can give you a clear yes on those specific things.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually find that your worries are about a wide range of everyday things, or are they more focused on specific areas like work or school?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just one thing. Work is a big piece of it, like I keep thinking the lab’s new partnership might somehow mean my position gets cut, or that my data are wrong and I’ve missed something obvious.\n\nBut then it jumps—like, bills, whether they’re going to pile up, whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, and then I start thinking I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school. But it’s been about seven months now, and since then my thoughts have just been… jumping more, and I keep feeling like I can’t get settled. I remember even small procedure changes turning into these long conversations in my head about what it meant, and then I’d go home and still be thinking about it while trying to do normal stuff. So, yeah, I’d say that’s when it really started.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're staying away from certain social situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, kind of. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because my mind just won’t stop jumping around. Like, one minute I’m thinking the new partnership at our lab is going to mean my position gets cut, and then I’m worried my data are wrong, and then it’s bills, and whether my family thinks I’m falling behind.\n\nAnd then somehow that turns into me thinking I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school. It’s kind of constant. I mean, I try to focus, but it just keeps moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do these jumping thoughts ever get stuck on a single idea or worry that you feel compelled to think about over and over?\n\nPatient: Not usually one single idea, I guess. It’s more like I try to tell myself, okay, just focus on finishing this report, or just check the assay in front of you. But then one worry kicks off another, and suddenly I’m staring at the screen while my head is going through every possible way things could go wrong.\n\nLike it starts with the report being late, then maybe the results are off, then what if someone notices, and then it’s not even about the report anymore. It just keeps branching.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do something specific over and over, like checking or repeating certain actions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? Not in a... ritual kind of way, if that’s what you mean. I mean, I’ll look back at something at work because I’m worried I missed something, but then I get pulled into thinking about deadlines or whether people are annoyed with me, or—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed myself needing to repeat some exact action over and over.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I probably put things off sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it avoiding whole situations. Like, I still go to work and do the lab stuff, even if I’m kind of in my head about it.\n\nSometimes I’ll take longer to start something because I’m thinking through everything that could be wrong with it, and then I end up reorganizing my notes or answering some email that isn’t even urgent. Sorry, that’s not exactly the same thing. I guess I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding specific places or activities because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worries and jumping thoughts are significantly affecting your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I kept wanting to say it’s just stress or I’m just behind, but I actually missed an internal lab deadline because I couldn’t get my writing and data review organized. I had the data, mostly, but I kept circling back through it and then the report just… didn’t come together.\n\nAnd it’s not only work. Even basic stuff feels like too much lately—getting reports ready, showing up for art nights with friends, cooking for my family. I’ll mean to do it, and then my head is full of all the things I need to sort out, and suddenly I’m behind on everything. So yeah, it’s affecting my life more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you're feeling anxious or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed, like, obvious sweating or trembling. Racing heart maybe? But I also drink too much coffee sometimes, especially when I’m trying to finish lab notes, so then I start wondering if it’s anxiety or just caffeine or because I skipped lunch, which I know is not helpful.\n\nWhen I’m overwhelmed it’s more like I realize I’m tense or kind of wound up, but I don’t always stop to check what my body is doing. So… I don’t know if I can give you a clear yes on those specific things.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually find that your worries are about a wide range of everyday things, or are they more focused on specific areas like work or school?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just one thing. Work is a big piece of it, like I keep thinking the lab’s new partnership might somehow mean my position gets cut, or that my data are wrong and I’ve missed something obvious.\n\nBut then it jumps—like, bills, whether they’re going to pile up, whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, and then I start thinking I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school. And then I’m checking emails or rereading numbers, and it all kind of blends together. So… yeah, it’s a pretty wide range, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a particular event or experience that you think might have triggered the start of these worries and jumping thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it started around the funding shift at the lab… when they brought in that commercial partnership and everything about how things were being run kind of changed.\n\nIt’s hard to pin it to one exact day, because at first I thought I was just adjusting, like everyone was. But it’s been about seven months now, and since then my thoughts have just been… jumping more, and I keep feeling like I can’t get settled. I remember even small procedure changes turning into these long conversations in my head about what it meant, and then I’d go home and still be thinking about it while trying to do normal stuff. So, yeah, I’d say that’s when it really started.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're staying away from certain social situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, kind of. It’s less like I’m actively saying, “I’m not going to social things,” and more like everything feels like too much to keep up with, so I just… don’t go.\n\nLike there’s this art night I used to show up for, and lately I keep missing it or bailing because I’m stuck trying to get reports together, or I’m rereading data, or I just can’t get myself organized. I actually missed an internal lab deadline because I couldn’t pull my writing and data review together, which was embarrassing, and after that even normal stuff—cooking for my family, preparing routine reports—started feeling like this huge pile of tasks.\n\nSo social things sort of fall off first, I guess. It’s not that I hate being around people. I just feel behind before I even get there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt an overwhelming urge to perform a specific behavior or mental act in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it an “overwhelming urge,” exactly. I remember even small procedure changes turning into these long conversations in my head about what it meant, and then I’d go home and still be thinking about it while trying to do normal stuff. So, yeah, I’d say that’s when it really started.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're staying away from certain social situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, kind of. It’s less like I’m actively saying, “I’m not going to social things,” and more like everything feels like too much to keep up with, so I just… don’t go.\n\nLike there’s this art night I used to show up for, and lately I keep missing it or bailing because I’m stuck trying to get reports together, or I’m rereading data, or I just can’t get myself organized. I actually missed an internal lab deadline because I couldn’t pull my writing and data review together, which was embarrassing, and after that even normal stuff—cooking for my family, preparing routine reports—started feeling like this huge pile of tasks.\n\nSo social things sort of fall off first, I guess. It’s not that I hate being around people. I just feel behind before I even get there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt an overwhelming urge to perform a specific behavior or mental act in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it an “overwhelming urge,” exactly. I mean, when I’m worried I’ll do things like get stuck thinking about it, but… specific behavior or mental act sounds kind of formal.\n\nLike, sometimes I’ll try to mentally line up everything I need to do, but then I end up thinking about something else, like whether I forgot to reply to someone, and then it turns into this whole thing about how I’m not managing my time well. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear specific thing like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant improvement in your worries and jumping thoughts when the situation that triggered them, such as the lab's funding shift, is not directly in front of you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, even when I’m not physically at the lab or nobody’s talking about the partnership, my mind just keeps jumping around.\n\nIt’ll start with, like, “What if the new partnership means my position gets cut?” but then it’s suddenly, “What if my data are wrong?” and then I’m thinking about bills piling up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind. And then somehow I’m on whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school, even though I might just be, I don’t know, brushing my teeth or trying to eat dinner.\n\nSo it doesn’t feel like it improves much just because the lab stuff isn’t right in front of me. It follows me around.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries and jumping thoughts would improve if the situation at the lab returned to how it was before the funding shift?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but… I don’t think it would just stop. The lab thing is what I keep circling back to, like whether this new partnership means my position gets cut, but then my brain jumps to whether my data are wrong, and then bills, and then my family thinking I’m falling behind.\n\nAnd then I start thinking I’ve already messed up grad school somehow, which—sorry, I know that wasn’t exactly the question. I guess what I mean is, even if the lab went back to normal, I feel like my mind would probably find the next thing to latch onto.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these persistent worries and jumping thoughts?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about seven months, I think. Around when the lab funding shifted and that commercial partnership started changing how everything was being run.\n\nAt first I thought it was just me reacting to the changes, because everyone was kind of tense, and the schedule felt different, and I kept telling myself I’d settle down once things were clearer. But it just kept going, and now it feels like it’s been most of the year, basically.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

It’s kind of constant. I mean, I try to focus, but it just keeps moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do these jumping thoughts ever get stuck on a single idea or worry that you feel compelled to think about over and over?\n\nPatient: Not usually one single idea, I guess. It’s more like I try to tell myself, okay, just focus on finishing this report, or just check the assay in front of you. It just keeps branching.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do something specific over and over, like checking or repeating certain actions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? Even basic stuff feels like too much lately—getting reports ready, showing up for art nights with friends, cooking for my family. I’ll mean to do it, and then my head is full of all the things I need to sort out, and suddenly I’m behind on everything. So yeah, it’s affecting my life more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you're feeling anxious or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed, like, obvious sweating or trembling. Racing heart maybe? But I also drink too much coffee sometimes, especially when I’m trying to finish lab notes, so then I start wondering if it’s anxiety or just caffeine or because I skipped lunch, which I know is not helpful.\n\nWhen I’m overwhelmed it’s more like I realize I’m tense or kind of wound up, but I don’t always stop to check what my body is doing. So… I don’t know if I can give you a clear yes on those specific things.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually find that your worries are about a wide range of everyday things, or are they more focused on specific areas like work or school?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just one thing. Work is a big piece of it, like I keep thinking the lab’s new partnership might somehow mean my position gets cut, or that my data are wrong and I’ve missed something obvious.\n\nBut then it jumps—like, bills, whether they’re going to pile up, whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, and then I start thinking I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school. And then I’m checking emails or rereading numbers, and it all kind of blends together. So… yeah, it’s a pretty wide range, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a particular event or experience that you think might have triggered the start of these worries and jumping thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it started around the funding shift at the lab… when they brought in that commercial partnership and everything about how things were being run kind of changed.\n\nIt’s hard to pin it to one exact day, because at first I thought I was just adjusting, like everyone was. But it’s been about seven months now, and since then my thoughts have just been… jumping more, and I keep feeling like I can’t get settled. I remember even small procedure changes turning into these long conversations in my head about what it meant, and then I’d go home and still be thinking about it while trying to do normal stuff. So, yeah, I’d say that’s when it really started.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're staying away from certain social situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, kind of. It’s less like I’m actively saying, “I’m not going to social things,” and more like everything feels like too much to keep up with, so I just… don’t go.\n\nLike there’s this art night I used to show up for, and lately I keep missing it or bailing because I’m stuck trying to get reports together, or I’m rereading data, or I just can’t get myself organized. I actually missed an internal lab deadline because I couldn’t pull my writing and data review together, which was embarrassing, and after that even normal stuff—cooking for my family, preparing routine reports—started feeling like this huge pile of tasks.\n\nSo social things sort of fall off first, I guess. It’s not that I hate being around people. I just feel behind before I even get there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt an overwhelming urge to perform a specific behavior or mental act in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it an “overwhelming urge,” exactly. I mean, when I’m worried I’ll do things like get stuck thinking about it, but… specific behavior or mental act sounds kind of formal.\n\nLike, sometimes I’ll try to mentally line up everything I need to do, but then I end up thinking about something else, like whether I forgot to reply to someone, and then it turns into this whole thing about how I’m not managing my time well. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear specific thing like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant improvement in your worries and jumping thoughts when the situation that triggered them, such as the lab's funding shift, is not directly in front of you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, even when I’m not physically at the lab or nobody’s talking about the partnership, my mind just keeps jumping around.\n\nIt’ll start with, like, “What if the new partnership means my position gets cut?” but then it’s suddenly, “What if my data are wrong?” and then I’m thinking about bills piling up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind. And then somehow I’m on whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school, even though I might just be, I don’t know, brushing my teeth or trying to eat dinner.\n\nSo it doesn’t feel like it improves much just because the lab stuff isn’t right in front of me. It follows me around.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries and jumping thoughts would improve if the situation at the lab returned to how it was before the funding shift?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but… I don’t think it would just stop. The lab thing is what I keep circling back to, like whether this new partnership means my position gets cut, but then my brain jumps to whether my data are wrong, and then bills, and then my family thinking I’m falling behind.\n\nAnd then I start thinking I’ve already messed up grad school somehow, which—sorry, I know that wasn’t exactly the question. I guess what I mean is, even if the lab went back to normal, I feel like my mind would probably find the next thing to latch onto.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these persistent worries and jumping thoughts?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about seven months, I think. Around when the lab funding shifted and that commercial partnership started changing how everything was being run.\n\nAt first I thought it was just me reacting to the changes, because everyone was kind of tense, and the schedule felt different, and I kept telling myself I’d settle down once things were clearer. But it just kept going, and now it feels like it’s been most of the year, basically.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these worries and jumping thoughts for more than six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, more than six months. It’s been around seven months now, starting when the lab funding shifted and that commercial partnership changed how everything was being run.\n\nI keep thinking, like, if I’d tracked it on a calendar maybe I could be more exact, but it was around then that it started feeling like my head wouldn’t quiet down. So… yeah, definitely past six months.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I mean, I try to focus, but it just keeps moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do these jumping thoughts ever get stuck on a single idea or worry that you feel compelled to think about over and over?\n\nPatient: Not usually one single idea, I guess. It’s more like I try to tell myself, okay, just focus on finishing this report, or just check the assay in front of you. But then one worry kicks off another, and suddenly I’m staring at the screen while my head is going through every possible way things could go wrong.\n\nLike it starts with the report being late, then maybe the results are off, then what if someone notices, and then it’s not even about the report anymore. It just keeps branching.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do something specific over and over, like checking or repeating certain actions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? Not in a... ritual kind of way, if that’s what you mean. I mean, I’ll look back at something at work because I’m worried I missed something, but then I get pulled into thinking about deadlines or whether people are annoyed with me, or—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed myself needing to repeat some exact action over and over.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I probably put things off sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it avoiding whole situations. Like, I still go to work and do the lab stuff, even if I’m kind of in my head about it.\n\nSometimes I’ll take longer to start something because I’m thinking through everything that could be wrong with it, and then I end up reorganizing my notes or answering some email that isn’t even urgent. Sorry, that’s not exactly the same thing. I guess I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding specific places or activities because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worries and jumping thoughts are significantly affecting your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I kept wanting to say it’s just stress or I’m just behind, but I actually missed an internal lab deadline because I couldn’t get my writing and data review organized. I had the data, mostly, but I kept circling back through it and then the report just… didn’t come together.\n\nAnd it’s not only work. Even basic stuff feels like too much lately—getting reports ready, showing up for art nights with friends, cooking for my family. I’ll mean to do it, and then my head is full of all the things I need to sort out, and suddenly I’m behind on everything. So yeah, it’s affecting my life more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you're feeling anxious or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed, like, obvious sweating or trembling. Racing heart maybe? But I also drink too much coffee sometimes, especially when I’m trying to finish lab notes, so then I start wondering if it’s anxiety or just caffeine or because I skipped lunch, which I know is not helpful.\n\nWhen I’m overwhelmed it’s more like I realize I’m tense or kind of wound up, but I don’t always stop to check what my body is doing. So… I don’t know if I can give you a clear yes on those specific things.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually find that your worries are about a wide range of everyday things, or are they more focused on specific areas like work or school?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just one thing. Work is a big piece of it, like I keep thinking the lab’s new partnership might somehow mean my position gets cut, or that my data are wrong and I’ve missed something obvious.\n\nBut then it jumps—like, bills, whether they’re going to pile up, whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, and then I start thinking I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school. And then I’m checking emails or rereading numbers, and it all kind of blends together. So… yeah, it’s a pretty wide range, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a particular event or experience that you think might have triggered the start of these worries and jumping thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it started around the funding shift at the lab… when they brought in that commercial partnership and everything about how things were being run kind of changed.\n\nIt’s hard to pin it to one exact day, because at first I thought I was just adjusting, like everyone was. But it’s been about seven months now, and since then my thoughts have just been… jumping more, and I keep feeling like I can’t get settled. I remember even small procedure changes turning into these long conversations in my head about what it meant, and then I’d go home and still be thinking about it while trying to do normal stuff. So, yeah, I’d say that’s when it really started.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're staying away from certain social situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, kind of. It’s less like I’m actively saying, “I’m not going to social things,” and more like everything feels like too much to keep up with, so I just… don’t go.\n\nLike there’s this art night I used to show up for, and lately I keep missing it or bailing because I’m stuck trying to get reports together, or I’m rereading data, or I just can’t get myself organized. I actually missed an internal lab deadline because I couldn’t pull my writing and data review together, which was embarrassing, and after that even normal stuff—cooking for my family, preparing routine reports—started feeling like this huge pile of tasks.\n\nSo social things sort of fall off first, I guess. It’s not that I hate being around people. I just feel behind before I even get there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt an overwhelming urge to perform a specific behavior or mental act in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it an “overwhelming urge,” exactly. I mean, when I’m worried I’ll do things like get stuck thinking about it, but… specific behavior or mental act sounds kind of formal.\n\nLike, sometimes I’ll try to mentally line up everything I need to do, but then I end up thinking about something else, like whether I forgot to reply to someone, and then it turns into this whole thing about how I’m not managing my time well. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear specific thing like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant improvement in your worries and jumping thoughts when the situation that triggered them, such as the lab's funding shift, is not directly in front of you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, even when I’m not physically at the lab or nobody’s talking about the partnership, my mind just keeps jumping around.\n\nIt’ll start with, like, “What if the new partnership means my position gets cut?” but then it’s suddenly, “What if my data are wrong?” and then I’m thinking about bills piling up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind. And then somehow I’m on whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school, even though I might just be, I don’t know, brushing my teeth or trying to eat dinner.\n\nSo it doesn’t feel like it improves much just because the lab stuff isn’t right in front of me. It follows me around.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries and jumping thoughts would improve if the situation at the lab returned to how it was before the funding shift?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but… I don’t think it would just stop. The lab thing is what I keep circling back to, like whether this new partnership means my position gets cut, but then my brain jumps to whether my data are wrong, and then bills, and then my family thinking I’m falling behind.\n\nAnd then I start thinking I’ve already messed up grad school somehow, which—sorry, I know that wasn’t exactly the question. I guess what I mean is, even if the lab went back to normal, I feel like my mind would probably find the next thing to latch onto.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these persistent worries and jumping thoughts?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about seven months, I think. Around when the lab funding shifted and that commercial partnership started changing how everything was being run.\n\nAt first I thought it was just me reacting to the changes, because everyone was kind of tense, and the schedule felt different, and I kept telling myself I’d settle down once things were clearer. But it just kept going, and now it feels like it’s been most of the year, basically.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these worries and jumping thoughts for more than six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, more than six months. It’s been around seven months now, starting when the lab funding shifted and that commercial partnership changed how everything was being run.\n\nI keep thinking, like, if I’d tracked it on a calendar maybe I could be more exact, but it was around then that it started feeling like my head wouldn’t quiet down. So… yeah, definitely past six months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these worries and jumping thoughts nearly every day for the past six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, nearly every day. It’s been about seven months now, since the lab’s funding shift and that commercial partnership started changing how everything was being run.\n\nI mean, some days I’ll think, okay, maybe today I’m more focused, and then I’ll be washing a beaker or looking at a spreadsheet and suddenly I’m back in it again. So… yes, for the past six months at least, pretty much daily.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. So… yeah, it’s a pretty wide range, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a particular event or experience that you think might have triggered the start of these worries and jumping thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it started around the funding shift at the lab… when they brought in that commercial partnership and everything about how things were being run kind of changed.\n\nIt’s hard to pin it to one exact day, because at first I thought I was just adjusting, like everyone was. But it’s been about seven months now, and since then my thoughts have just been… jumping more, and I keep feeling like I can’t get settled. I remember even small procedure changes turning into these long conversations in my head about what it meant, and then I’d go home and still be thinking about it while trying to do normal stuff. So, yeah, I’d say that’s when it really started.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're staying away from certain social situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, kind of. It’s less like I’m actively saying, “I’m not going to social things,” and more like everything feels like too much to keep up with, so I just… don’t go.\n\nLike there’s this art night I used to show up for, and lately I keep missing it or bailing because I’m stuck trying to get reports together, or I’m rereading data, or I just can’t get myself organized. I actually missed an internal lab deadline because I couldn’t pull my writing and data review together, which was embarrassing, and after that even normal stuff—cooking for my family, preparing routine reports—started feeling like this huge pile of tasks.\n\nSo social things sort of fall off first, I guess. It’s not that I hate being around people. I just feel behind before I even get there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt an overwhelming urge to perform a specific behavior or mental act in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it an “overwhelming urge,” exactly. I mean, when I’m worried I’ll do things like get stuck thinking about it, but… specific behavior or mental act sounds kind of formal.\n\nLike, sometimes I’ll try to mentally line up everything I need to do, but then I end up thinking about something else, like whether I forgot to reply to someone, and then it turns into this whole thing about how I’m not managing my time well. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear specific thing like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant improvement in your worries and jumping thoughts when the situation that triggered them, such as the lab's funding shift, is not directly in front of you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, even when I’m not physically at the lab or nobody’s talking about the partnership, my mind just keeps jumping around.\n\nIt’ll start with, like, “What if the new partnership means my position gets cut?” but then it’s suddenly, “What if my data are wrong?” and then I’m thinking about bills piling up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind. And then somehow I’m on whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school, even though I might just be, I don’t know, brushing my teeth or trying to eat dinner.\n\nSo it doesn’t feel like it improves much just because the lab stuff isn’t right in front of me. It follows me around.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries and jumping thoughts would improve if the situation at the lab returned to how it was before the funding shift?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but… I don’t think it would just stop. The lab thing is what I keep circling back to, like whether this new partnership means my position gets cut, but then my brain jumps to whether my data are wrong, and then bills, and then my family thinking I’m falling behind.\n\nAnd then I start thinking I’ve already messed up grad school somehow, which—sorry, I know that wasn’t exactly the question. I guess what I mean is, even if the lab went back to normal, I feel like my mind would probably find the next thing to latch onto.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these persistent worries and jumping thoughts?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about seven months, I think. Around when the lab funding shifted and that commercial partnership started changing how everything was being run.\n\nAt first I thought it was just me reacting to the changes, because everyone was kind of tense, and the schedule felt different, and I kept telling myself I’d settle down once things were clearer. But it just kept going, and now it feels like it’s been most of the year, basically.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these worries and jumping thoughts for more than six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, more than six months. It’s been around seven months now, starting when the lab funding shifted and that commercial partnership changed how everything was being run.\n\nI keep thinking, like, if I’d tracked it on a calendar maybe I could be more exact, but it was around then that it started feeling like my head wouldn’t quiet down. So… yeah, definitely past six months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these worries and jumping thoughts nearly every day for the past six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, nearly every day. It’s been about seven months now, since the lab’s funding shift and that commercial partnership started changing how everything was being run.\n\nI mean, some days I’ll think, okay, maybe today I’m more focused, and then I’ll be washing a beaker or looking at a spreadsheet and suddenly I’m back in it again. So… yes, for the past six months at least, pretty much daily.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain situations because you fear they might trigger these worries or jumping thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve sat down and thought, “I’m avoiding this because it’ll set me off,” exactly.\n\nSometimes I’ll kind of… take the longer route around things, if that makes sense, but then I get caught up thinking about whether that even counts as avoidance. Like in the lab, people avoid certain benches just because someone spilled acetone there once, and then it becomes this whole unspoken map everyone follows—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern like that. I’d probably need to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control your worries most of the time, or do they feel like they're controlling you?\n\nPatient: It feels more like they’re controlling me, honestly. I’ll try to tell myself, okay, just focus on this one thing—finish the report, check the assay, whatever—and then one worry kind of sets off another.\n\nLike I’ll be staring at the screen, and instead of actually working, my head is running through every possible way something could go wrong. And then I start thinking about how much time I’m wasting thinking, which becomes its own thing, and… yeah. It doesn’t feel like I can just shut it off most of the time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… a lot. It’s not just that it feels bad in my head, it’s started messing with things I’m supposed to actually do.

I missed an internal lab deadline because I couldn’t get my writing and data review organized. I kept opening the same files and sort of circling them, like when you keep re-labeling samples because the label angle looks wrong, except it wasn’t useful, it was just me getting stuck.

And now even normal stuff feels too big sometimes—preparing reports, showing up for art nights with friends, cooking for my family. Those shouldn’t feel impossible, but lately it feels like I can’t keep up with any of it. So yes, it’s distressing.

It’s definitely not just work, though work is kind of the easiest place to point to. Like, I’ll start with worrying about whether this new lab partnership means my position might get cut, and then it jumps to whether my data are wrong, and then somehow I’m thinking about bills piling up.

I actually missed an internal lab deadline because I couldn’t pull my writing and data review together, which was embarrassing, and after that even normal stuff—cooking for my family, preparing routine reports—started feeling like this huge pile of tasks.\n\nSo social things sort of fall off first, I guess. It’s not that I hate being around people. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear specific thing like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant improvement in your worries and jumping thoughts when the situation that triggered them, such as the lab's funding shift, is not directly in front of you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, even when I’m not physically at the lab or nobody’s talking about the partnership, my mind just keeps jumping around.\n\nIt’ll start with, like, “What if the new partnership means my position gets cut?” but then it’s suddenly, “What if my data are wrong?” and then I’m thinking about bills piling up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind. I just feel behind before I even get there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt an overwhelming urge to perform a specific behavior or mental act in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it an “overwhelming urge,” exactly. I mean, when I’m worried I’ll do things like get stuck thinking about it, but… specific behavior or mental act sounds kind of formal.\n\nLike, sometimes I’ll try to mentally line up everything I need to do, but then I end up thinking about something else, like whether I forgot to reply to someone, and then it turns into this whole thing about how I’m not managing my time well. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear specific thing like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant improvement in your worries and jumping thoughts when the situation that triggered them, such as the lab's funding shift, is not directly in front of you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, even when I’m not physically at the lab or nobody’s talking about the partnership, my mind just keeps jumping around.\n\nIt’ll start with, like, “What if the new partnership means my position gets cut?” but then it’s suddenly, “What if my data are wrong?” and then I’m thinking about bills piling up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind. And then somehow I’m on whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school, even though I might just be, I don’t know, brushing my teeth or trying to eat dinner.\n\nSo it doesn’t feel like it improves much just because the lab stuff isn’t right in front of me. It follows me around.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries and jumping thoughts would improve if the situation at the lab returned to how it was before the funding shift?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but… I don’t think it would just stop. The lab thing is what I keep circling back to, like whether this new partnership means my position gets cut, but then my brain jumps to whether my data are wrong, and then bills, and then my family thinking I’m falling behind.\n\nAnd then I start thinking I’ve already messed up grad school somehow, which—sorry, I know that wasn’t exactly the question. I guess what I mean is, even if the lab went back to normal, I feel like my mind would probably find the next thing to latch onto.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these persistent worries and jumping thoughts?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about seven months, I think. Around when the lab funding shifted and that commercial partnership started changing how everything was being run.\n\nAt first I thought it was just me reacting to the changes, because everyone was kind of tense, and the schedule felt different, and I kept telling myself I’d settle down once things were clearer. But it just kept going, and now it feels like it’s been most of the year, basically.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these worries and jumping thoughts for more than six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, more than six months. It’s been around seven months now, starting when the lab funding shifted and that commercial partnership changed how everything was being run.\n\nI keep thinking, like, if I’d tracked it on a calendar maybe I could be more exact, but it was around then that it started feeling like my head wouldn’t quiet down. So… yeah, definitely past six months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these worries and jumping thoughts nearly every day for the past six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, nearly every day. It’s been about seven months now, since the lab’s funding shift and that commercial partnership started changing how everything was being run.\n\nI mean, some days I’ll think, okay, maybe today I’m more focused, and then I’ll be washing a beaker or looking at a spreadsheet and suddenly I’m back in it again. So… yes, for the past six months at least, pretty much daily.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain situations because you fear they might trigger these worries or jumping thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve sat down and thought, “I’m avoiding this because it’ll set me off,” exactly.\n\nSometimes I’ll kind of… take the longer route around things, if that makes sense, but then I get caught up thinking about whether that even counts as avoidance. Like in the lab, people avoid certain benches just because someone spilled acetone there once, and then it becomes this whole unspoken map everyone follows—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern like that. I’d probably need to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control your worries most of the time, or do they feel like they're controlling you?\n\nPatient: It feels more like they’re controlling me, honestly. I’ll try to tell myself, okay, just focus on this one thing—finish the report, check the assay, whatever—and then one worry kind of sets off another.\n\nLike I’ll be staring at the screen, and instead of actually working, my head is running through every possible way something could go wrong. And then I start thinking about how much time I’m wasting thinking, which becomes its own thing, and… yeah. It doesn’t feel like I can just shut it off most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a lot. It’s not just that it feels bad in my head, it’s started messing with things I’m supposed to actually do.\n\nI missed an internal lab deadline because I couldn’t get my writing and data review organized. I kept opening the same files and sort of circling them, like when you keep re-labeling samples because the label angle looks wrong, except it wasn’t useful, it was just me getting stuck.\n\nAnd now even normal stuff feels too big sometimes—preparing reports, showing up for art nights with friends, cooking for my family. Those shouldn’t feel impossible, but lately it feels like I can’t keep up with any of it. So yes, it’s distressing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries are mainly about one area of your life, like work, or are they spread across many different areas?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just work, though work is kind of the easiest place to point to.
